    8.4 Technical Appendix

 Loss Control Procedure

 Loss of fluid returns will lower the hydrostatic head of the drilling fluid in the wellbore, thereby
  inducing a kick. The influx fluid will then flow to the surface or into the zone of lower pressure.
  Lost circulation can occur in naturally occurring fractured, vuggy, cavernous, sub-normally
  pressured or pressure depleted formations. Induced losses can occur from mechanical fracturing
 due to pressure surges while breaking circulation. In all cases of lost circulation, attempts should
 be made to keep the hole full. The hole can be filled with either light drilling fluid or water. A
  record of the amount of fluid pumped should be made.

 The concentration of LCM will vary according to losses. In the event of seepage losses, treat
 system with a finer grade of LCM at 20 – 30 Kg/m³. In the event of moderate to high rate of well
  bore  loss, prepare  pills  at different graded concentrations and spot over  thief zone. The
  concentration may vary between 18 to 70 lb/bbl. In order to avoid LCM plugging MWD tools a
  circulation sub should be installed in the drill string. The MWD engineer is to advise on the size
 and concentration of LCM that can be pumped through MWD Tools. Ensure that the drilling
  displace the LCM pill out of the string prior to shutting down the mud pumps.

 Important Note: Please note below concentration and recommended materials may be
 change with same materials on rig site.

  In case of having circulation losses:

 Losses less than 10 bbls/hr:

               -   Active system to be loaded with 10 lb/bbl of Fiber Fine.

 Losses 10- 40 bbls/hr:

               -   Active mud to be loaded with 15- 20 lb/bbl of Fiber Medium.

               -  Sweep the hole frequently with 50 bbls high viscous pill contains;

               -  15 lb/bbl Mica Medium - Corse.

               -  10 lb/bbl Walnut Shells Medium -Fine.

               -  10 lb/bbl Oyster Shell Fine.

 Losses 40 – 100 bbl/hr:

               -   Stop Drilling.

               -   Pull out string above the loss zone

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 116 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

               -   Spot 100 bbls pill contains;

               -  30 lb/bbl Fiber coarse.

               -  30 lb/bbl Walnut Fine.

               -  20 lb/bbl Mica Med.

               -   Repeat the previous pill minimum of two times.

 Losses more than 100 bbls:

               -  Up to company procedure, may be squeeze 50 bbls of Cement squeeze.

        8.4.1.1  Mixing LCM Pill

 The amount of LCM that can be added to a mud depends very much on the mud properties. High
  gels and YP will help keep the material in suspension. Since LCM is generally of low gradient,
  excess material tends to float on top if too much is added. The following procedure may prove
  useful for mixing LCM pills and avoiding the problem of flotation.

      1. ENSURE the pill has enough viscosity to keep the low gradient material in suspension
         before addition of LCM. ADD appropriate viscosifier if required.
      2. ADD the material in stages e g. 5, 10, 15, 20 etc. ppb.
      3. STOP after desired concentration has been reached, or when it is observed that the material
        cannot be held in suspension
      4.  If a higher LCM content is desired, LEAVE the pill to soak for a period of time and then
      TRY ADDING more material.

        8.4.1.2 LCM Pill Spotting Procedure

      1.  After spotting LCM pill, pull the bit above the calculated top of the pill. Wait 30minutes
        and monitor the annulus.
      2.  After 30 minutes, attempt to fill the annulus, if not already full.  If the hole can be filled,
         close the BOP and squeeze 10 bbls of LCM pill into the formation without exceeding 200
           psi.
      3.  If the hole remains stable, continue to drill ahead.
      4.  Should the LCM pill fail to reduce the losses or the hole cannot be filled, then mix and spot
         another 50 bbls of LCM pill.
      5.  Attempt to fill the annulus.  If successful, close the BOP and squeeze 20 bbls of LCM pill
          into the formation without exceeding 200 psi.  If a squeeze pressure develops, hold the
         pressure for 1 hour before staging the drill string back to bottom.

       Notes:

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 117 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

               -   All time hole must be full, transfer mud to trip tank and asked driller to fill hole
              from trip tank.

               -   All LCM pills must be thoroughly mixed and homogenised before being pumped.

               -  When pumping an LCM pill through a bit with nozzle smaller than 18/64, DO
          NOT USED LCM Medium and Coarse sizes.

               -  Remove filter screens from the drill pipe and if necessary from the mud pump
                suction lines.

               -   This is particularly true if the mud density is high, 14 lb/gal or greater. Fine loss
                 circulation material which does not adsorb a lot of water should be used in
              weighted mud.

 Guide Line for Fiel Engineers for Shale Inhibition

  Shale Hydration takes two forms in water-sensitive shales: surface and osmotic adsorption.
  Surface hydration occurs when a small volume of water is strongly adsorbed onto the planar
  surfaces of the clays which causes little softening or swelling, but can lead to excessive stresses
  if the swelling is confined.

 Osmotic swelling occurs when a large volume of weakly held water is attracted to the clay
  surfaces by electrostatic forces. Osmotic swelling causes softening and significant swelling as the
  adjacent clay layers hydrate water and expand. Osmostic swelling does not generate excessive
  stresses, even when confined, and can be substantially reduced if Extremely difficult drilling
  conditions have led to more systematic approaches to analysing wellbore instabilities.

 One such approach that can be used as a guideline for the mud engineer involves three basic steps:

      1.  Monitor and analyse the fundamental failure mechanism.
      2.  Determine and understand the factors governing this failure mechanism.
      3. Model the problem considering impact of proposed changes.

        8.4.1.3  Monitor & Analyze the Failure Mechanism

 Monitoring of data and events on the rig is the first step of the process and involves:

         • Collection, organization and processing of drilling data. Drilling data such as Rate of
       Penetration (ROP), Revolutions per Minute (RPM), Weight-on-Bit (WOB), Bottom-Hole
      Assembly (BHA), torque, over pull Trip In Hole (TIH) and Pull Out Of Hole (POOH), swab
      and surge, flow rate, mud weight, mud Rehology , etc. should be collected, organized and
       analyzed on a continual basis and compared to the symptoms of wellbore stability being
       observed. This will help evaluate performance and establish trends or any deviations and
      measure the effectiveness of treatments and changes.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 118 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

         • Formation characterization. The standard procedure in drilling operations is to apply
       the knowledge

  gained from previous wells to subsequent wells drilled in similar geologic regions. Wellbore-
  instability

  investigation requires evaluation of shales during the drilling process. Since rig site shale testing
  is limited

 due to equipment and time limitations, most evaluations are performed in a properly equipped
  laboratory.

  Laboratory  tests are conducted to evaluate shale swelling,  dispersibility, cation exchange
  capacity, hydration capacity, colloidal content, capillary suction time, shale hardness tests and
 mud compatibility properties.

 A number of methods have been used to perform rig site tests. These include tests as simple as
  the methylene blue test (CEC) measurement of shale; shale hardness measurements; hot-rolled
  dispersion testing with various mud formulations and additives; and sophisticated analytical
 measurements of various clay properties. It is particularly important to try and determine the
  failure mechanism: swelling and softening or brittle spalling. Information from these tests, current
  shaker observations and mud logger

  data are used to evaluate drilling fluid performance, adjust mechanical drilling parameters and
 making casing design decisions. The primary options to counter shale problems at the rig site are:

       Change the drilling fluid density.

       Change to an oil- or synthetic-base system.

       Change to a more inhibited water-base mud.

       Reduce fluid loss to very low levels and increase filtrate viscosity.

        Utilize pore-plugging additives, emulsified insoluble materials or plastering agents.

       Adjust mechanical drilling parameters.

       Evaluation and analysis of instability symptoms.

  Instability warning signs such as the presence of excessive cavings on the shakers for a given
 ROP, fill on connections, tripping difficulties, torque, stuck pipe, etc. indicate that something is
 wrong down hole. The magnitude or volume of failed rock should be estimated and recorded with

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 119 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

  the other drilling parameters. The number and location of specific incidents should be recorded,
 such as pipe

  sticking, fill or packing-off, reaming and re-drilling. All of this information can then be organized
 and correlated with time and depth to detect the location and quantify the severity of critical
  sections in the well.

        8.4.1.4  Determine & Understand Failure Mechanism

  All of the monitored and plotted parameters are then evaluated to identify the most probable
 mechanism causing the instability. The most useful symptoms used to indicate the mechanism
  responsible for wellbore instability are:

        Cavings analysis.

       Tripping difficulties.

        Drilling conditions.

       Mud system analysis.

        In-situ conditions.

  These symptoms should be evaluated based on an interdisciplinary knowledge of the causes of
  wellbore instability to determine the most likely failure mechanism.

        8.4.1.5  Model & Considering Proposed Changes

 Once the monitoring and determination steps have produced parameters, organize and analyze
  this data to obtain qualitative solutions and evaluate proposed changes. One good way to do this
  is through the use of a decision tree or flowchart approach to evaluate the possible failure
 mechanism and to identify the most promising remedies. After arriving at a proposed solution, a
  corrective measure should be applied and

  the results used as feedback into the method and decision tree method.

 A decision tree or flowchart, such as shown in Figure 9 for evaluating the cause of excessive
  cavings, provides a logical path for identification of the mechanism and a qualitative solution.
  Maintaining hole stability while drilling shale sections can be particularly troublesome. Simple
  solutions do not always exist, but good drilling practices combined with good mud practices are
 most often successful. Analysis of the mechanical stress/strain relationships has helped to develop
  the proper density requirements for hole stability.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 120 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

    8.5 Directional Drilling

  Drilling Fluid Properties & Selection

  It is common knowledge among drilling engineers and drillers that borehole problems can be
  considerably more severe in a directional hole than in a vertical well. The problems are magnified
  as the hole angle and horizontal displacement increases. Although most of these reactions are
 mechanical in nature, much can be done to alleviate them by proper selection and maintenance of
  the drilling fluid. For example, if a water-base fluid is being used, lubricants can be added to
 lower the coefficient of friction between the drill string and borehole or casing. Inhibiting salts
 can be added to reduce the interaction of the drilling fluid with the exposed formations, thereby
 improving borehole stability.

 There are a number of drilling factors influenced by the drilling fluid, but the following are of
 primary concern in drilling a horizontal well.

       Borehole instability

       Hole cleaning

       Torque and drag

       Formation damage

        Barite sag

       Hydraulics

 Borehole Instability

 Borehole instability is one of the more costly, but relatively common problem associated with
  drilling oil and gas wells. Not only can it impede drilling, it can result in loss of the hole. The
 term normally conjures thoughts of borehole collapse with accompanying stuck pipe. This is one
  of the more critical reactions of borehole instability, but it can manifest itself in more subtle ways
 such as hole-fill, bridges, hole enlargement, or hole size reduction.

 These reactions lead to other associated problems including increased torque and drag, logging
  difficulties, poor cement jobs, and increased drilling days, all of which add significantly to the
  drilling cost. It should be pointed out that the discussions to follow do not cover hole enlargement
 problems from weakly consolidated sands, or rubble zones that erode from the hydraulic action
  of the drilling fluid.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 121 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

  Stabilization

 The swelling activity is controlled by adjusting the electrolyte content of the fluid to minimize
 expansion of the clays. Lime and gypsum-treated fluids have long been used for this purpose, but
  basically do not satisfy the inhibition needs of the more troublesome shales. The potassium ion is
 an effective swelling inhibitor and is used extensively for this purpose through the addition of
 potassium salts. The concentration of potassium utilized ranges from several hundred mg/L to
  over 60,000 mg/L. The most cost-effective potassium concentration, however, appears to be in
  the 3000 to 7000 mg/L range. In marine environments, seawater or seawater with additional
 sodium chloride, are good alternates to potassium. The tendency for the shale to disperse or
  subdivide into discrete particles appears to be best controlled by the addition of an adsorptive,
  high molecular weight water-soluble polymer.

 One of the more effective polymers for this purpose is the partially hydrolyzed poly acrylamide
  with molecular weight in excess of 15 million. These anionic polymers adsorb on to the surface
  of the cuttings and borehole wall to act as a binder or elastic coating to retard dispersion. Another
 approach to stabilizing the hard brittle shales that contain natural or induced micro fractures is the
  use of water-dispersible asphalt or asphaltines. The addition of these type materials appear to plug
  the micro-fractures, or perhaps even surface pore throats to retard further fluid invasion. This
  action has proven very effective in certain shales and can be applied in most water base fluids.

 Hole Cleaning

 Cleaning cuttings from a vertical hole has been covered extensively by numerous investigators.
 Hole cleaning in a vertical hole is essentially a matter of overcoming the slip velocity of the
  cuttings with sufficient annular velocity and carrying capacity of the drilling fluid. Although any
  velocity greater than the settling velocity of the largest cutting will theoretically lift all the cuttings
  to surface eventually, too low an annular velocity will lead to an undesirable high concentration
  of cuttings in the annulus. Experience has shown that cuttings concentrations more than 5% by
 volume cause tight hole or stuck pipe when circulation is stopped.

 Cuttings Bed Formation

  Radial slip velocity increases with increasing angle of inclination, forcing more cuttings to the
 low side of the annulus, which eventually results in the formation of a cuttings bed. reported that
 bed formation was dramatic in the 40° to 50° angle with the bed sliding downward against the
  flow, resulting in a very high cuttings concentration.

 The fact that a cutting bed forms is a serious problem, but the instability of the bed in this critical
  angle of the well makes the problem even more severe. High torque and drag resulting from the
  high cutting concentration at the lower part of the angle can obviously result in stuck pipe or loss
  of circulation.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 122 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

 Hole Cleaning Sweeps

  In static drill string concluded that high-density sweeps were more effective than high-density
  high-viscosity sweeps based on field measurements. The higher viscosity of the latter caused flow
  diversion to the high side of the wellbore. Not surprisingly, high-viscosity sweeps also performed
  poorly. Also concluded that the best hole cleaning sweeps were high-density low viscosity
  sweeps, with a density 3 – 4 ppg higher than that of the drilling fluid. the following procedure is
 recommended:

       Calculate sweep height in the vertical part of the annulus, and added hydrostatic head in
         the annulus.

       Rotate at 80+ rpm while pumping the sweep, 180+ rpm is preferred. It is not recommended
         to pump a weighted sweep while sliding.

       Keep pumping until the sweep is out of the hole. If pumping stops while the sweep is in
         the deviated hole, it falls out of the “jet stream” and re-deposits captured fines.

       Avoid increasing the viscosity of the weighted sweep.

       Plan to run 3 – 4 sweeps to achieve desired results when hole cleaning problems are
         indicated. One sweep will probably not be sufficient to remedy an existing situation.

       Avoid checking for flow or closing the annulus with the weighted sweep in the drill pipe.

        If desired for preventative maintenance: 1) schedule sweeps every 6 – 8 hours or 500 –
       1000 feet (whichever comes first, unless the hole dictates greater frequency); barite/silt
         build- ups are dependent on time and footage: and 2) pump a sweep while circulating
        bottom up before tripping, and another once back on bottom.

 concluded that high-density sweeps were more effective than high-viscosity sweeps.

 They strongly advocated the use of sweep reports, which should be standard practice in any
  drilling fluids operation.

 Short Tripping

 Another technique that has proven helpful in controlling problems created by the cuttings bed in
  the high-angle portion of the well is to make periodic short trips. Tripping the bit through the bed
  not only assures that the bottom hole assembly and bit can be pulled through it, but the action
  should agitate the bed to the point that a portion will slide down the hole and subsequently be
  picked up when circulation is commenced. The time intervals for these short trips will be
 determined by the torque and drag conditions that exist in any particular well. Lacking specific
  well history, a general rule of thumb is to short trip at least once for every 500 feet of new hole.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 123 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

  Drill String Rotation

 The industry is in total agreement that drill string rotation improves hole cleaning, especially in
  hole sizes less than 17 ½”. The general opinion in the industry is that the optimum drill string
  rotation rate is approximately 120 rpm. It is believed that the drill string rotation improves hole
  cleaning by mechanically disturbing the cuttings bed on the low side of the hole. There could also
 be effects due to skewing the velocity profile in the wellbore and the creation of Taylor Vortices.
 Encouraged the use of rotary steerable systems.

 Turbulance

 Bottom hole assembly design becomes important because the flow restrictions of some of the
 components, such as the measurement-while-drilling tools or positive displacement motors, may
  not allow sufficient flow to reach turbulence in the annulus. Turbulence can, of course, be induced
  at some constant flow rate by decreasing the annular area. This may be more difficult to
 accomplish in a horizontal well because of limitations on the design of drill strings and tool
  assemblies. An increase in the effective viscosity of the drilling fluid will decrease the Reynolds
 Number, and thus increase the flow rate required to obtain turbulent flow. Thus, a lower effective
  viscosity fluid may be desirable in the horizontal well, but attention must also be given to cleaning
  the larger diameter portion of the upper hole or possible washed-out sections where  it  is
  impractical to maintain turbulent flow.

  It is interesting to point out a basic fact between static fluid pressure and dynamic fluid pressure.
  Static fluid pressure is a function of true vertical depth, while dynamic fluid pressure, or ECD, is
  a function of measured depth. Thus, the ECD will continue to increase, albeit slowly, even after
  the hole becomes essentially horizontal. The fracture pressure could be reached if the hole was
 extended to sufficient depth and the fluid density is close to the fracture gradient.

  Hole Cleaning Guideline

 The single most important factor related to hole cleaning in deviated wells is flow rate. During
  directional drilling operations drilled cuttings will settle on the low side of the hole and form a
  stationary bed if insufficient annular fluid velocity is used. The critical flow rate required to
  prevent cuttings bed formation must be determined .

  Typically few hole cleaning problems exist in vertical or horizontal sections. Most problems

  associated with hole cleaning are seen on deviated wells and occur in the 40 – 60 degree section
 where gravity effects can cause cuttings beds to slump down the hole.Typical flow rates to aim
  for in ERD wells:

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 124 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

         Hole Size                           Typical Flow Rates

           17 ½”    800 gpm minimum. Some rigs achieve 850 – 1000 gpm.

                  Aim for 800 gpm (although 800 – 1000 gpm is typically achieved). If
           12 ¼”     800 gpm is not achievable, ensure tripping procedures are in place for
                        poorly cleaned hole.

           8 ½”    Aim for 400 gpm.

 When correctly designed, both laminar and turbulent flow regimes will effectively clean a
  deviated well. In general increasing the viscosity of a fluid in laminar flow will improve hole
  cleaning as will a reduction of the viscosity of a fluid in turbulent flow. It is important that one or
  other regime is selected and that the transition zone between the two is avoided.

  Generally viscous fluids in laminar flow are preferred because:

        It is possible to achieve higher cleaning capacity.

       Viscous fluids give better transport in the near-vertical sections.

       Viscous fluids have better suspension characteristics when circulation is stopped.

        It is difficult to achieve “turbulent flow” except in small hole sizes.

 Experience has shown that good drilling fluid rheological properties are extremely important to
  hole cleaning when drilling a high angle well. Studies show that the effects of drilling fluid

  rheological properties and annular flow regime are mutually dependent. In the laminar flow
  regime, increasing fluid viscosity will improve hole cleaning and this is particularly effective if
  the Yield Stress is high. In the turbulent regime, however, reducing fluid viscosity will help
 removing cuttings. Therefore the drilling fluid rheological properties should be designed to avoid
  the transitional flow regime. For hole sizes above 8 ½”, the annular flow is laminar under most
  circumstances. Therefore it is necessary to specify a minimum Yield Stress. The optimum level
  required is best established based on field data and experience.

 A higher ROP requires a higher flow rate to clean the hole. It is good practice to drill the hole
  with a steady ROP and select the required flow rate for hole cleaning accordingly. Before making
  a connection, the hole should be circulated at the normal flow rate to clear the cuttings from
 around the BHA. Depending upon the hole angle and length of BHA, a circulation time of 5 to
 10 minutes is often required.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 125 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

 Before tripping out, the hole should be circulated at the normal flow rate until the shakers are
  clean, and the drill pipe be rotated at maximum speed/reciprocated in the meantime. This may
  require up to 3 bottoms-ups, depending on the hole angle and hole size. Below are listed the
 recommended number of bottoms-up prior to tripping.

                 Hole Angle,
                                 8 ½”       12 ¼”       17 ½”
                     deg

                    0 – 10           1.3           1.3            1.5

                   10 – 30           1.4           1.4            1.7

                   30 – 60           1.6           1.8            2.5

                    60+             1.7           2.0            3.0

  Overall friction can become so great in an extended-reach well or horizontal section that the drill
  string can only be rotated with great difficulty. Needless to say, this condition is conducive to
  stuck pipe.  Obviously,  this  coefficient  of  friction  will be  significantly  affected by  the
  lubricating properties of the drilling fluid.

 any of the causes of torque and drag are mechanical, while others are related to hole geometry.
  Several, however, can be directly attributed to the drilling fluid. Since wall contact with the drill
  string will be extremely high  in a horizontal hole,  it  is imperative  that the lubricating
  characteristics of the fluid be considered.

  Formation Damage

 The obvious reason for drilling a horizontal well is to expose more of the producing zone to the
  wellbore for increased production. Thus, the importance of preventing formation damage during
  drilling and completion of the horizontal well is just as important as it is in its vertical counterpart.

  In fact, it may be more important since the increased cost of drilling the horizontal well must
 show additional production for its justification. A 50% damage or permeability loss in a potential
 500 bbl/day horizontal well represents a considerably greater loss in production than a 50%
 damage in a 100 bbl/day vertical hole through the same reservoir.

 Sandstone production is by far the most susceptible reservoir rock to damage or permeability loss
  during drilling and completion operations. Though formation damage may be caused by poor
  drilling and completion practices, one of the primary causes is due to exposure to the drilling and
 completion fluid.

  This loss, attributable to the fluid, can be caused by one or more of the following occurrences:

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 126 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

        Particle transport from the drilling fluid into the production zone, subsequently plugging
        pore throats.

       Fluid filtrate reacting with expandable clays in the rock to decrease pore throat diameters.

        Particle movement within the permeable rock due to dispersion of clays and other minerals
        from quartz surfaces.

        Wettability changes of the formation from exposure to the drilling fluid filtrate.

        Interaction of fluid filtrate with formation fluids to form water - insoluble precipitants.

 The effect of these actions on the permeability of sandstones has been the subject of a considerable
  investigation. In almost all cases, some damage is caused by the drilling and completion fluid.
 The primary objective is to limit this damage as much as possible. Consequently, selection of the
  drilling and completion fluid becomes an extremely important function in drilling the horizontal
  well.

  Recommended Fluid Parameters

       Velocity – One of the major obstacles to overcome in horizontal and ERD drilling is hole
         cleaning. In vertical holes, cuttings have room to settle without causing problems. In
         horizontal wells, cuttings only have to settle 4+ inches to form a cuttings bed. Research
        and practical experience has shown that cuttings bed formation is analogous to sand dunes
       on an ocean beach. The bed moves in a wave fashion up the hole. If sufficient annular
         velocity is not present, the bed thickens until it reaches equilibrium, which usually occurs
       when turbulent flow conditions exist. With water, turbulent flow is easily obtained since
         viscosity is in the denominator of the turbulent flow equation, and water has a viscosity
         of 1. Fluids other than water require more pump output to approach turbulent flow
         conditions. Practical experience in other industries has proven that materials can be
         transported in water slurries if the fluid velocity is between 3 and 7 ft/sec.

       Rheological Properties – Ideally one would want a true thixotropic fluid to  drill
         horizontally. In effect, two different conditions exist simultaneously. One occurs in the
          vertical portion of the hole where larger diameter casing may be set, or wash-outs in the
        open hole occur. In that case, a fluid having a high Yield Stress, or if the fluid is visco-
          elastic, the initial gel strength needs to be elevated. The opposite is needed in the
         horizontal section from a hole-cleaning point of view. Here one wants water for the
         reasons pointed out earlier. Compromises are the result. If casing is set into the zone of
          interest, or if formations up-hole are not a problem, then water may be possibly combined
        with occasional sweeps.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 127 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

       Surface Apparatus – Special attention must be placed on the solids removal equipment at
         the surface. Solids removal efficiency must be continually monitored so that unwanted
          particulate material is not retained in the fluid system that may negatively affect fluid
        weight or filter cake quality, resulting in lost circulation or increased torque and drag. The
         cost of problems that may occur due to not doing this correctly may far outweigh the cost
         of equipment needed.

    8.6 Hydrogen Sulphide Gas in Drilling Fluid

 Description of Hydrogen Sulphide Gas

 Hydrogen sulphide is a colourless gas, with an offensive odour and a sweetish taste. It is soluble
  in water, alcohol, oils, and many other solvents. It has a specific gravity of 1.1895 with reference
  to air. It is considered a weak acid, it is toxic to humans and corrosive to metals. Hydrogen
  sulphide can be dangerous to personnel on the surface as it is extremely toxic to human and even
 animal life, and is extremely corrosive to most metals as it can cause cracking of drill pipe and
  tubular goods, and destruction of testing tools and wire lines. When hydrogen sulphide enters the
  borehole during  drilling, completion, or testing for hydrocarbons,  it creates several very
  detrimental problems. These problems are encountered regardless of the source of the hydrogen
  sulphide. Hydrogen sulphide has created unsolved problems in the oil field.

 Origin of Hydroge Sulphide

 During oil and gas operations, hydrogen sulphide can be found as a component of formation gases,
  dissolved in water,  hydrocarbons, or even liquid sulphur. Thermal degradation of organic
  materials and sulphate reducing bacteria (SRB) can create hydrogen sulphide along with other
  gases.

 Problem Cused by Hydroge Sulphide Gas

        8.6.1.1  Health & Safety of the Personnel

 One of the main problems hydrogen sulphide causes is in respect to the health and safety and
  safety of the personnel. A maximum of eight hours exposure to concentrations greater than 100
 ppm H2S will cause hemorrhage and death. Concentrations above 600 ppm can be fatal in three
  to five minutes. Highly stressed, high strength steel can fail in a matter of minutes in the presence
  of 50 ppm H2S. At high pressure, as little as 0.1 ppm H2S can greatly reduce the time to failure
  of highly stressed, high strength steel. Relying solely on its odour is not a good idea because at
  concentrations above 100 ppm it deadens a person’s sense of smell within a few minutes. The
  pure gas is heavier than air and can collect in low areas such as pit rooms and accommodation.
 below Table shows the potential hazards hydrogen sulphide can cause to the personnel from short-
 term exposures. There are many historical cases to document the ill effects of hydrogen sulphide
  exposure.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 128 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

 The documented cases reported in Table 2 indicate that hydrogen sulphide is very toxic and it
  takes only a matter of minutes to affect unprotected workers.

                 Health effects from short-term exposure to hydrogen sulphide

            Concentration
                                                Health Effect
              (ppm)
             0.01 – 0.3           Odour threshold
                                   Offensive odour, possible nausea, tearing of the eyes             1 – 20
                                     or headaches with prolonged exposure
                                  Nose, throat and long irritation; digestive upset and
                                        loss of appetite; sense of smell starts to become            20 – 50

        8.6.1.2   Effects of Hydrogen Sulphide on Metal

 Hydrogen sulphide mainly cause corrosion problems to drill strings, transport pipes, storage tanks
  etc. Hydrogen sulphide causes sulphide stress cracking, hydrogen embrittlement and pitting
  corrosion in oil and gas operations. The corrosion of iron in the presence of hydrogen sulphide
 and water is dependent upon the dissociation of the hydrogen-sulphide molecule. Iron is oxidized
  to the ferrous form at the anode and hydrogen sulphide under- goes a two-step dissociation at the
  cathode. Tung et al22propose the following equilibrium reactions below.

 At the anode:

 Fe    Fe2+ + 2e-

 And at the cathode hydrogen is produced and either enters the steel or forms hydrogen gas and
  bubbles off:

  If H2S is present:

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 129 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

 2H+ + 2e-   H° + H°

  If H2S is not present:

 H° + H°         H2

 Hydrogen enters the steel first by adsorption onto the water to steel interface and then by being
 absorbed into the steel as hydrogen. The amount of hydrogen entry depends on the corrosion rate
  of the steel surface and the concentration of anions such as HS- that reduce the tendency to
 produce hydrogen gas (as shown in second equation above) and promotes the hydrogen (Ho) to
  enter the steel. pH also has an influence on amount of corrosion damage. Basically, the term
  “sulphides” used in oil and gas operations includes all three water-soluble species, H2S, HS- and
  S2-, which coexist in a sulphide-water system. Shown in the ionization chart for hydrogen
  sulphide in figure 1, it can be observed that molecular H2S predominates in the acidic range,
 where the pH is below 7. In the range of 7 to 12, monovalent HS- predominates and above pH 12,
  divalent S2- predominates.

 The illustration in figure 1 indicates that the most important practice is that when H2S enters in
  alkaline mud, it reacts to form an alkaline sulphide, most often sodium sulphide.17 While elevated
 pH is a good way to keep H2S from causing problems under certain conditions, it does not remove
  it from the fluid and any drop in pH can create a significant hazard. This then brought about the
  advent of sulphide scavengers in the history of oil and gas operations. The only safe method for
  the total removal of hydrogen sulphide or soluble sulphides is with a sulphide scavenger.

        8.6.1.1   Effects of Hydrogen Sulphide on Drilling Fluids

 The first positive identification of soluble sulphide in the drilling fluids must be taken as a possible
  indication of H2S entering the fluid from some source. In the simplest case an increase in mud
 weight may be sufficient to control minor influxes. If levels persist chemical treatment will be
  required. This can take two forms; neutralization and scavenging. The two terms must not be
  confused. Neutralization of H2S in drilling fluid by caustic soda or lime is a common field
  approach. This alkalinity maintenance method has proved successful when small amounts of H2S
  enter the drilling fluid, for example, from drilling up pore spaces containing the gas.

  This is not a suitable approach to the control on influxes of Hydrogen Sulphide. The alkalinity
  neutralization of H2S is instantly reversible by reduction in pH. Should the pH of the fluid
  decrease due to further influxes of H2S, Co2 or brine flows the sulphids may come out of solution
  as hydrogen sulphide gas. To ensure that such influxes are handled safely, it is essential to employ
  a sulphide scavenger. This term refers to any additive which is capable of reacting with one or
 more of the sulphide species and as a result, converting them to a more inert form. To be truly
  effective an irreversible and complete reaction must occur. As a mutual equilibrium exist between
  the three sulphide species in solution (H2S, HS‐ and S2‐) irreversible and complete removal of
 one serves to removal all three.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 130 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

 To remove the problem  it is necessary to precipitate the sulphide as an insoluble inorganic
  sulphide. Zinc or iron compounds are usually chosen because of the relative inertness of the
  sulphids formed. Zinc source (Zinc oxide or carbonate) has in the past been the preferred
  treatment. However,  continuous  treatment  with Zinc  carbonate  will produce  significant
  undesirable carbonate concentration (Carbonate contamination) which can adversely effect on
  drilling fluid rehology and fluid loss. See the below table:

     Contaminan  FV   PV  YP   Gels  FL   pH  Pm    Pf   Mf    Cl‐   Ca2
               t                                                                      +
      Carbonate

       H2S

                     Table 8-1 Quick reference for recognizing carbonate and H2S contamination

 As a precaution against Hydrogen Sulphide influxes into the drilling fluid system should be pre‐
  treated with Zinc Source. Zinc oxide can be used as an alternative, provided that the possible
  environmental payloads associated with its use are appreciated. pH and alkalinity tests (Pf/Mf)
  are a good first indicator of Hydrogen Sulphide influx. However, the use of scavenger is used up.
  It can be seen from this that while a scavenger increase safety levels, it also makes detection of
  small Hydrogen Sulphide influxes difficult by conventional methods. Measurement of the
  concentration of scavenger in the mud system is possible but the analysis method is not safe for
  use on the rig.

 The most effective environmentally acceptable scavengers for Hydrogen Sulphide in water base
 muds are Zinc sources like Zinc Carbonate or Oxide, primarily because of their solubility which
  leads to very fast reaction rates compared to other scavengers.

 Zinc Sources clearly reacts very much faster than the other scavengers. This is due to the fact that
  the iron in solution ensures a fast reaction with the sulphides and the chemistry in this case
 produces elemental sulphur as the reaction product. Elemental sulphur is completely insoluble in
  water. The iron is reduced from Fe+3 to Fe+2 in the process and some of this will be regenerated
  to produce Fe+3 when the mud comes in to contact with the air. Any regeneration of Fe+3 will
  aid in reducing product consumption without affecting is scavenging properties.

 Recommended treatment levels of Zinc sources are as follows:

  pre‐ emptive treatment is recommended at 1.0 lb/bbl treatment of a mud system in the event of
  gas influx should be made on the basis that 2.0 lb/bb; will treat approximately 100 ppm Hydrogen
  Sulphide.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 131 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

        8.6.1.1   Testing, Reporting, Treatment of Hydrogen Sulphide

 The active mud system must be regularly analyzed and monitored for the presence of active
  soluble Sulphide with the Garrett Gas train. This test must be carried out at the same time as each
 complete mud check, i.e.: three times per day while drilling.

  Alkalinity checks should be carried out  intermittently in between these  full checks and
 any significant reduction in the pH and Mf/Pf should be followed up by testing for the presence
  of active soluble sulphides with the Garrett Gas Train.

 For routine checks lead acetate paper can be used to give a qualitative check. Should this test be
  positive then it must be followed by a qualitative analysis with a Dragger tube.

 The following actions must be taken after the initial positive test result:

      1.  Inform the Drilling Supervisor
      2.  Start adding H2S Scavenger to the active mud system. Active soluble sulphides can be
        analysed and monitored because of their characteristic of readily regenerating hydrogen
        Sulphide gas in reaction with mild acid. By adding citric acid to whole mud sample in a
         Garrett gas train detection of Hydrogen Sulphide on lead acetate paper indicates the
        presence of active Sulphides in the mud. When this qualitative test proves positive a
        dragger tube should be used to give qualitative results.

 However it must be remembered that pre‐treating the mud with MILGARD Fe means that no
  Sulphides will be delectable until all the scavenger has reacted, i.e.: it is impossible to detect small
  influxes of Hydrogen Sulphide in a system pre‐ treating with MILGARD Fe .

        8.6.1.1  Contingency Stock to be Kept on Hand on the Rig

 The following Contingency stock of treatment chemicals must be kept on hand on the rig over
 and above the normal mud maintenance material requirements.

 Zinc Carbonate or Oxide 120 Sxs 25 kg

 Lime 200 Sxs 25 kg

 As above mentioned Hydrogen Sulphide is a poisonous and dangerous acidic gas encountered in
 many formations. It can quickly deaden senses and can be fatal, even at low concentration.
 Hydrogen Sulphide is characterized by its typical “rotten egg” smell. For safety reasons, it should
 be neutralized immediately with caustic soda or lime to increase pH to >11.5 to form sulphide
  (S2‐), then precipitated with a source of Zinc.

  Neutralization with caustic soda:

 2 NaOH + H2S 2Na+ + S2‐ + 2H2O (at pH>11.5)

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 132 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

  Neutralization with maintaining excess lime:

 2 Ca(OH)2 + H2S Ca2+ + S2‐ + 2H2O (at pH>11.5)

 Converting Hydrogen Sulphide into just sulphide by increasing pH is not a permanent reaction.
  If the pH were drop into the acid region, sulphide will be converting back into the poisonous H2S
  form. For this reasons H2S should always be precipitated by adding a source of Zinc such as Zinc
 Oxide.

 Removal by precipitation with treatments of (ZnO):

 H2S + ZnO ZnS + H2O

  if H2S gas detected in mud following action should be taken to:

      3.  Immediately rise up the pH to at least 11.5 to 12 with caustic soda.
      4.  Buffer the pH with lime.
      5.  Begin treatment with Zinc oxide to remove sulphide (S2‐) from the system. (1 lb/bbl
         treatment of Zinc oxide removes approximately 1100 mg/lit of sulphide. No more than 2
         lb/bbl of Zinc oxide should be used to pre‐treat the system for H2S)

  If it appears that H2S bleeding in (flowing) form the formation, the mud density should be
  increased to shut off the flow of gas into the well‐bore.

 Hazard & Risk Assessment

 The use of chemicals in the workplace presents hazards and risks to personnel involved in their
  handling and application. In order to minimize these hazards risk assessments are performed and
 HSE control measures and management systems are established to achieve the following:-

     Identify hazards
       Safely manage those hazards.

     Identify risks
       Where possible, eliminate those  risks through control  / engineering measures  e.g.
         ventilation and collection of dust.
       Where not possible, manage those risks through processes  including the use of PPE.
       Provide training and awareness systems designed to achieve the above and promote
        continuous improvement.

 Hazardous Materials

       The effect on a person of a hazardous material depends on:
       The nature of the hazardous material.
       The site of the action.
       The amount of the hazardous material involved (“dose”).
       The reaction of the individual (“susceptibility”).

    8.8 Completion Section

                           Completion Fluid Formulation

           Component          Conc. (ppb)          Component Description

       Calcium Bromide (14.2ppg)    0.984 bbl/bbl                 Base Fluid

           Corrosion Inhibitor       1% Vol                    Anti-Corrosion

        H2S Scavenger Liquid      0.5% Vol        H2S Removal/ Anti Corrosion

        Oxygen Scavenger CA         0.5 ppb         Oxygen Removal/Anto Corrosion

        8.8.1.1  DRILLING FLUID FORMULATION

  Kill Mud Mixing Procedure:

      1.  Fill tanks with calculated amount of Drill Water 0.576bbl./bbl.
      2.  Treat Drill Water with Caustic soda and Soda Ash by 0.3pbb
      3. Mix NaCl at 66ppb.
      4. Add Xanthan-Gum by 0.2pbb.
      5. Add CaCO3-F (325ppb) to reach MW of ±106pcf

     In case of foaming use Alcohol-Based Defoamer.

 Note: Use Hi Vis pill to clean hole if necessary.
  Calculation is based on per bbl. of mud.
 Completion Fluid Mixing Procedure:

      1. Make sure that all pits and lines involved with completion fluid are thoroughly cleaned,
         flushed and free of any fluid and solid.

     Make sure that at least one large pit is kept empty for transferring ready brine into after
            given time for settling. After transferring one pit to empty pit, the first one is to be
            flushed and cleaned for accommodating the brine of next ready pit, and so on to the
               last pit.

     Depends on the rig facilities and structure, mud engineer on board may find it better
             to prepare the fluids pit-by-pit.

      2.  Transfer the content of CaBr2 drums into designated pits.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 138 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

     Record the number of drums poured in each tank for accurate volume in it as the base
            of calculation for adding other additives into; do not rely on pit gauge, sounding or
         mud logging sensor, but have a look on them for comparison and/or correlation if
           found useful.

      3. Mix Corrosion Inhibitor into pits at concentration of 3.5pbb (1% by volume).
      4. Mix H2S Scavenger Liquid into pits at concentration of 0.5% by Volume.
      5. Do not add Oxygen Scavenger CA at this stage as it should be added directly from the top
         of the pit, just prior to pump the fluid into the well.
      6.  Shut down all agitators and mixing pumps, and leave the mixed fluid in the pits for at least
       12 hours to let any solid particle inside (mostly insoluble Lime) settles down.
      7.  Start transferring the clear fluid of the first pit to the empty pit by means of an air-powered
       Wilden pump.

     Make sure that the suction hose of the Wilden pump is not put deep close to the bottom
            of the pit, otherwise, settled solids are sucked in.

      8.  After finishing the first pit, stop Wilden pump, wash, flush and clean the pit, use it as the
       empty pit for accommodating the fluid of second pit using the and same above method.

 Note: all CaBr2 brine inside the well must be retrieved by the time of displacing the well by kill
 mud. In case of need, filtration must be applied to retrieved amount of CaBr2.
 Based on well readings and the operation, mud weight may be varied.

        8.8.1.2 DOWNHOLE LOSSES

 As perforation had been done in well AZN#85 & there is exposure to reservoir, risk of any
  probable formation losses must be taken into consideration.
 Losses will be minimized/avoided by the planned use of properly sized bridging/weight material
  (and Polymer) in conjunction with appropriate drilling practices. If seepage losses are noted on
  the well no specific action will be taken but close monitoring of the loss rate is to be made. For
 moderate loss rates addition of CaCO3 50 micron and 150 micron is recommended to ensure that
  there is adequate correctly sized material to act as bridging agents.

 Seepage losses Less than 10 bbl. /hr.

 No specific action will be taken, but close monitoring of the probable loss rate is made.

 Losses from 10-30 bbl. /hr.

    Load CaCO3-M (150-300 micron) in system up to 5ppb

  *If loss not cured and even tends to increase, then:
      Pump 40-60bbl of pill made by 7-10ppb CaCO3 150-300μ & 7-10ppb CaCO3 600μ

 Losses from 30-50 bbl. /hr.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 139 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

    Load CaCO3-M (150-300 micron) in system up to 5ppb
    Since some of the CaCO3-M will be discarded over the shakers, CaCO3-M to compensate
         the separated amount on the shakers.

  *If loss not cured and even tends to increase, then:
      Pump 40-60bbl of pill made by
       10-20ppb CaCO3 200-300μ
       10-20ppb CaCO3 600 μ
       10pbb Oyster Shell-F
       10pbb Oyster Shell-M.

 Losses from 50-100 bbl/hr.

    Load CaCO3-M (150-300 micron) in system up to 5ppb
    Load CaCO3-C (600-100 micron) in system up to 5ppb
    Since some of the CaCO3-M&C will be discarded over the shakers, add CaCO3-M&C to
        compensate the separated amount on the shakers.

  *If loss not cured and even tends to increase, then:
    Pump 40-60bbl of the following pill:
       10-20ppb CaCO3 200-300 micron
       10-20ppb CaCO3 600 micron
       7-10ppb of each CaCO3 1000 micron & CaCO3 2000 micron
       5-10pbb Oyster Shell -F
       5-10pbb Oyster Shell -M

 Losses more than 100 bbl/hr.

 High concentrated LCM pill of various sizes of Calcium Carbonate and Oyster Shell-F&M&C
  should be pumped and spotted and gently squeezed over the loss zone.

  Spotting Procedure

      1.  After spotting LCM pill, pull the bit above the calculated top of the pill. Wait 30 minutes
        and monitor the annulus.
      2.  After 30 minutes, attempt to fill the annulus, if not already full. If the hole can be filled,
         close BOP and squeeze 10bbl LCM pill into formation without exceeding 200psi.
      3.  If the hole remains stable, continue to drill ahead.
      4.  Should the LCM pill fail to reduce the losses or the hole cannot be filled, then mix and
         spot another 50bbl of LCM pill.
      5.  Attempt to fill the annulus. If successful, close the BOP and squeeze 20bbl of LCM pill
         into the formation without exceeding 200psi.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 140 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

      6.  If a squeeze pressure develops, hold the pressure for 1 hour before running the drill string
        back to bottom.
 Notes:

     LCM pills must be thoroughly homogenised and viscous enough before being pumped.

     Pumping high-concentrated LCM pill is subject to use of open-end DP or Mule-Shoe.

     Remove filter screens from the drill pipe (if there is any) and if necessary, remove
              strainers from the mud pump suction lines.

        8.8.1.3  ENGINEERING COMMENTS

 Keep watching and control on well to record loss.
  All CaBr2 brine inside the well must be retrieved by the time of displacing the well by kill mud.
  In case of need, filtration must be applied to retrieved amount of CaBr2.

        8.8.1.4  DRILLING ISSUES

 There is no drilling objective in AZN-85 workover well; mud should be compatible with reservoir
 and work over operation.
  Solid Control equipment

      Use shale shaker to remove debris and LCM particle in case of use.
       Personal Safety

        8.8.1.5 MUD TYPE

  Inhibited CaBr2 brine (Calcium Bromide Brine) will be applied as completion fluid. And simple
  salt saturated kill mud to kill the well and clean out inside 9 5/8”, 7” Liner.

 Displacement Design
 The combined effect of scrapers being run in the hole, the high flow rate will help to
  remove/disperse general debris.
 At least 150m of pill/spacer coverage or 3 min contact time at the highest pump rates should be
  considered.
  Saturated CaCl2 brine can be applied as spacer/Hi-Vis pill while displacing.
  Pit levels should be monitor throughout the displacement operation to catch and prevent an influx
  entering the well contributing to an incident.
  All CaBr2 brine inside the well must be retrieved by the time of displacing the well by kill mud.
  In case of need, filtration must be applied to retrieved amount of CaBr2.

           All CaBr2 retrieved from well must be stored in separated tanks. All CaBr2 left on
           surface, even dead volume of tanks, must be transferred back to drums at end of well.

           duration.
               It is recommended to use desander tank as active pit of CaBr2 to minimize dead volume
         and return path.

    10. Casing Program

      10.1  Material Selection

      Casing, Liner and Tubing Material selection for South Azadegan oilfield will be
      describe in the following.

     Corrosive Environment Analysis and Strength Check

     Based on the data of offset wells in  this  oilfield, there  is a certain degree
      of  corrosive problems  in  the 4  layers  to be produced.  Corrosive medium
       is  principally composed  of 2  parts: one  is  the  corrosive  gases  like H2S
     and CO2 existing in the produced  fluid; and the other  is the high  salinity
      formation water. The contents of main  corrosive  fluids  in  offset wells  are
     shown in below Table:

          1)  Corrosion in this oilfield is a result of combined action of H2S, CO2 and
            high salinity formation water. The corrosive mechanism is complicated, and
             the corrosive rate on the casing is difficult to determine. Based on the NACE
             standard and the data from the Table, the producers result in severe corrosive
            environment. However, by the research on development conditions and
              technical indices, it can be found that although the corrosive fluid contents
              are high in the oilfield, the corrosive environment is not extremely serious
             as the following reasons:

          2)  In general, the temperature that cause the SSC/SCC is concentrated in the
            range from 20℃ to 30℃. The average depth of the horizontal section is
           2,730m with the reservoir temperature of 96℃, and tensional stress on the

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 148 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

             casing does not exist in the horizontal section. Therefore, the influence of
          SSC/SCC may not be considered for the casing/liner.

          3) Based on the RMDP, the production GOR in the first development phase
            should not be higher than the initial GOR, therefore, the flowing pressure is
             higher than the bubble point pressure all the time, that is, degassing problems
           do not exist in the horizontal section and some vertical section, the H2S and
         CO2 have no chance of separation out of oil phase, and  it is lack of
              prerequisite for casing corrosion.

          4) Based on above all, the corrosive environment of the casing string is not
              severe. The optimal selection for the casing string can be stated briefly as
              follows.

     Material Selection of Casing String

            In  this  oilfield,  the  corrosion  of  production  string  obviously  occurs
             in  2  regions. One  is  the  region  where  the  pressure  is  above  the
           bubble  point;  in  which  degassing  problems  do  not  exist,  and  the
            corrosion environment  is  relatively weak. Another one  is  the  region
          where the pressure  is below the bubble point; because of the decline
            of  pressure,  the  concentration  of  corrosive gas  will  increase and  the
            corrosion     is    relatively    serious.    Considering    the    corrosive
           environment  is not serious in the low water cut production stage and
            the production practice in this oilfield has proved the L80  is  fit for  it,
           So, the L80 should be taken as the material of 9-5/8″  casing,  7″, 5
            1/2″ and 4-1/2″  liner.  In order  to prevent  the hazard of gas leakage
             after production degassing  occurs,  9-5/8″  casing, 7″ and  4-1/2″  liner
           should be Gas tight thread. 2) The 110S  is selected as the material of
            9-5/8″, 7″ and4-1/2″ casing rather than L-80 due to the high strength
           requirement  in Fahliyan  wells, what’s more  the 110S  is  fit  for sour
           gas environment

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 149 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

Wellhead Material Selection Criteria

   A.  Selection of material class of Wellhead & X-mas Tree:

                                   Table 11-2: H2S & CO2 Content in AZNS Offset Wells

               Well        Reservoir      H2S from PVT     CO2 from PVT
             No         formation         (mol%)            (mol%)

           AZN2         Sarvak               0.13                   6.71
                              Sarvak               0.06                   5.44
           AZN4       Kuzhdumi             0                    2.62
                          Gadvan                                      2.64
           AZN6        Gadvan                                      2.47

   Considering the information above, API 6A 19th & NACE MR-0175 (2003) /ISO 1516, and Cl-
    content which is being most concerned, the South AZADEGA X-mas tree material and the tubing
   hanger should be FF0.5 class. As to the casing head, it’s barely exposed to resident fluid, or only
    for quite a short time, when it comes to desert working condition, according to API 6A19th &
   NACE-MR-0175 (2003) /ISO 1516, the tubing spool should be EE0.5, the casing head material
    class should be DD0.5 (ANSI 4130) , stainless steel cladding in ring groove

C.  Selection of PR (Performance Requirement)

According to API Specification 6A 19th edition, performance requirements are specific and unique
to the product in the as-shipped condition. All products shall be designed to perform according to
the requirements of API 6A clauses 4 and clauses 10 while in the pressure and temperature ranges
and corrosion environment. Other requirements include load capability, cycles, and operating force
or torque. There are two performance requirement levels: PR1 and PR2. Valves operating as safety
valves shall be of the PR2 performance level and meet the requirements of Annex I in API 6A For
SA wells, the guideline for PR selection is that all parts not contacting well fluids in normal
production period are PR1, all parts contacting well fluids are PR2 as follow, Casing head housing
and casing head spool assembly - PR1. Tubing head spool, tubing hanger and X-mas tree - PR2.

D. Temperature Class

Environment temp:
Well temp: 0.0244×4100+27.384=127℃
According to API 6A 19th, temp should be U (-18℃-121℃)

  12. Waste Management Program

    12.1   Drilling Waste Management Design

  The contractor is responsible for designing the optimum practices for treating the produced
   waste during the operation. The initial technical waste management proposal should be
   included the practices for minimizing, treating and recovering of the wastes.
   According to the below categories:
  .     Water based mud containing minerals and natural synthetic polymers, and
  .      Cuttings contaminated with WBM.

  The contractor is encouraged to offer possible practices to minimize the drilling fluids waste
  and its regeneration.

   Water based mud containing minerals, naturals and synthetic polymers

  .     Primary operation

  The proposed primary solids control package should be able to control drilling fluid properties,
   reducing of mud  materials usage  against conventional  solid  control  equipment's, and
   significantly reduce the number of excess fluids generated throughout drilling each phase of
   the well.

  .     Secondary Operation
   All excess mud and spillage (water based) should be collected in rig site pits and free surface
   fluid should be treated using a built for propose flocculation station (has to join with centrifuge)
   consisting of integral dosing and pumps along with a centrifuge for efficient, quick and
   controlled separation of the solids before the cleaned fluids is returned to active system or
   buffer tanks.

   This system should be able to remove colloidal sized particles that cannot be removed with
   primary solids control system.

   This system may use to clean up the remaining water-based mud or decrease weight of oil base
  mud at the end of the drilling fluids programmed.

   11.1.2 Cuttings contaminated with WBM
  Spud mud and water-based mud cuttings should be fixed and be ready for transport to disposal
   zone. For fixing the cutting, 100-150 kg cement shall be used for each cubic meters of cutting
   as well as 3-4-liter Sodium Silicate for each cubic meter of cutting. (Consumption of cement
  and sodium silicate varies in different formations). Gypsum is not allowed for cutting fixation.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 173 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

   This method is physically Bonds Contaminants to Prevent Leaching to Environment. Final
   product will pass can test and in case of approval by company man it’ll send to landfill.

    12.8  Waste management policy

  Waste management services should be utilized to minimize generated waste and contaminated
   discharged cuttings during drilling operations. This helps keep the environment clean and
   ensures that our operations are in compliance with DOE (Department of Environment)
   legislations.
  We provide cost-effective and optimum solutions from environmentally acceptable procedures
   to achieve zero-discharge plans and improve fluids recovery. The outcome reduces operational
   costs and enables recycling.
  The waste is analyzed to study various parameters such as chloride, nitrate, and phosphate.
   Other analysis is carried out to monitor treatment processes on a regular basis during waste
   generation. Our waste management personnel are fully trained experienced and have the

    High Performance Filter Unit

       Filtration plays an important role in the natural treatment of waste water as it percolates
      through the soil. It is also a major part of most water treatment. Groundwater that has
     been softened, or treated through iron and manganese oxidation, requires filtration to
     remove floc created by coagulation or oxidation processes. Since surface water is subject
      to run-off and does not undergo natural filtration.
         it must be filtered to remove particles and impurities. Filtration can be compared to a
      sieve or micro- strainer that traps suspended material between the grains of filter media.
     However, since most suspended particles can easily pass through the spaces between
      grains of the filter media, straining is the least important process in filtration. Filtration
      primarily depends on a combination of complex physical and chemical mechanisms, the
     most important being adsorption. Adsorption is the process of particles sticking onto the
      surface of the individual filter grains or onto the previously deposited materials. Forces
       that attract and hold particles to the grains are the same as those that work in coagulation
     and flocculation. In fact, coagulation and flocculation may occur in the filter bed,
      especially if coagulation and flocculation before filtration was not properly controlled.
      Incomplete coagulation can cause serious problems in filter operation.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 182 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

                                     Figure 12-6 _ High Performance Filter Unit.

    13. Health, Safety and Environment

      13.1   General  Quality,  Health,  Safety &  Environmental
           Requirements

       PPL/PEDEC’s committed to conducting operations in a manner which
             minimizes the risks to the health and safety of those involved in, and affected
               by, such operations. PPL/PEDEC’s also committed to conducting its
               operations in a manner which minimizes the impact on natural and ‘built’
             environments in the area of operations.
       This section sets out minimum Quality, Health, Safety and Environmental
           (QHSE) requirements for the WORK that MSA and subcontractors will be
               required to comply with whilst conducting operations for PPL/PEDEC.
       PPL/PEDEC may perform audits and assessments of MSA’s and its
               subcontractors’ performance under the CONTRACT. PPL/PEDEC reserves
               the right without prior notice to conduct quality, health, safety, security and
              environmental audits of the operations.
       MSA shall at all times be responsible for performing the WORK in a manner
              so as to protect against loss of life, injury to people, damage to property and
               the environment. The detailed manner means and methods of performing the
        WORK shall be under the control and direction of MSA.
       Prior to the start of Operations, certain QHSE processes must be completed.
             These QHSE processes include developing the final QHSE plan format and
              frequency of any QHSE audits, permitting and licensing and PEDEC’s
               approvals. MSA shall ensure that sufficient resources are dedicated to this task
                to ensure completion prior to mobilization.
       PPL/PEDEC may at any time stop the WORK or any portion of the WORK
              without penalty to PPL/PEDE Cif the WORK is not conducted in accordance
              with the Quality, Health, Safety or Environmental guidelines specified in this
         CONTRACT or by the laws of the appropriate jurisdiction. PPL/PEDEC
               reserves the right to require MSA to remove any person from the operations in
               the case of non-compliance with QHSE procedures. PPL/PEDEC may also
               prohibit the use of equipment and plant in cases where that equipment or plant
             does not meet the required standards or is deemed unsafe.
       MSA shall ensure that activities associated with this CONTRACT are
             conducted in compliance with applicable national and local HSE laws and
               regulations, MSA’s standards and PEDEC’s requirements and IADC
             documents.

────────────────────────────────────────────
────────────────────────────────────────────
             South Azadegan Oilfield Project

 Contract No:          AZNS-127 Re-Entry#01 Program               Page 188 of 229CCA/C/1401/27

       Limits of Authority
       PEDEC’s Representative(s) do not have the authority to relax health, safety or
             environment standards. If they judge the operation to be inadequate with
              regards to any aspect of quality, health, safety, security or the environment,
              then the operation may be immediately suspended.
       MSA shall be required to develop a project-specific QHSE plan to manage the
        WORK and associated risks. This plan shall, as a minimum, address topics
              included in the list detailed in Item 7. Where existing MSA QHSE
               publications detail the topics listed, it is sufficient to either include them
              verbatim, or provide reference to where they may be found in MSA’s QHSE
             Manuals. The final project-specific QHSE plan shall satisfy MSA standards,
           PPL/PEDEC requirements and applicable HSE laws and regulations.
       MSA should establish an effective HSE system and implement it strictly.
       MSA must adhere to the standards and regulations of Health, Safe and

       MSA must strictly adhere to the procedures / standards and regulations of

       MSA should comply with all applicable local government regulations
              including guidelines and recommended practices and any other Iranian
                authorities having jurisdiction.

               the indigenous population of the Iran and South Azadegan region.

      13.3   Environmental Management

       COMPANY shall comply with any and all laws, rules and regulations of
             governmental agencies having  jurisdiction, which now  exist or may be
             promulgated during the term of the CONTRACT, relating to the control and
              prevention of damage to the environment.
       Without prejudice to above, COMPANY shall ensure that all personnel use the
               highest prevailing standards of care and diligence to prevent unlawful disposal
               of all waste oil, water and other waste materials or fluids which may accumulate
               or be caused by COMPANY’s performance of the CONTRACT. COMPANY
                shall not dispose of any objects, scrap, waste, rubbish, equipment, fluids or fuel
               into the surrounding area or allow anything to be stored loose where it can be
              driven into surrounding areas by wind. COMPANY shall take all reasonable
              precautions to prevent pollution of any nature or kind from its performance of
               the WORK. COMPANY shall have contingency plans and procedures in place
                for such events.
       In the event of any disposal prohibited by Item 3.2 above, whether by dumping,
                 spilling, emission or otherwise arising from COMPANY’s performance or non-
             performance of the CONTRACT, COMPANY shall be fully liable for and bear
               the costs of removal of any such item(s) dumped, spilled, emitted or otherwise
              disposed regardless of the sole, concurrent or contributory negligence or breach
               of duty (statutory or otherwise) of PPL/PEDEC. In the event of any such
               disposal, COMPANY shall immediately undertake all reasonable remedial and
               protective measures required in such circumstances.
       COMPANY  shall  notify PPL/PEDEC immediately with  respect  to any
                pollution, loss, damage, claim or demand (or occurrence which may give rise
                to the same) resulting from the CONTRACT. COMPANY shall report to
           PPL/PEDEC any incidents of non-compliance with legislative and regulatory
              environmental requirements that occur during the performance of the WORK.
             Without relieving COMPANY of any of its obligations under Item 3.2, if
        COMPANY does not promptly proceed to perform its obligations under this
             Item 3, PPL/PEDEC may take part to any degree it deems necessary in the
               control and removal of any pollution, dumping, spillage or contamination which
                  is the responsibility of COMPANY under this Item 3.2, or which is due to any
                act or omission of COMPANY. COMPANY shall reimburse PPL/PEDEC for
               costs arising from such actions of PPL/PEDEC upon receipt of invoice from
           PPL/PEDEC. PPL/PEDEC shall have the right to deduct such costs from any
           sum due or becoming due to COMPANY under the CONTRACT.

       Well site shall be flat and clean. All the equipment, materials, drilling tools shall

       local community are recommended.

       Facilities of environment protection supplied to well site:

       In drilling operation keep mud and sewage from overflowing. If it happens,
              clean up timely.

       Mud chemicals shall be stored according to the standard and shall not be
               scattered around well site. If mud chemicals scatter while loading or
              unloading, clean it up at once. The sack of mud chemicals shall be recovered.
          Do not dispose it on site.
       Enhance water management at well location and do not wash equipment with
               fresh water. Stop water flowing for long time. Overhead tanks shall be
                 utilized.
       Oil management shall be carefully at the well site. No oil dirt on site, no
              dripping and leaking at oil tank and manifold system area.
       After the well is completed, reinforce the sewage pit ，solidify the disposed
          mud and have environment procedure fulfilled.
       Install fuel supply lines and keep them in good condition.
       All the drilling fluid materials shall be recovered and shall not be left at the
              well site after completion.

                shall check and accept the well site environment according to the regulation
                 after completion. Only well site environment is qualified, the well can be
               delivered.
       Transition Area for Hazardous Waste shall also be provided by each
        COMPANY in his allocated area,
       PPL/PEDEC shall approve each area before use as well. The transition area of
             hazardous waste shall be:
         Paved, fenced, marked and illuminated
         Equipped with appropriate Fire Extinguisher and other emergency response
         equipment (e.g., Eye-Wash and Emergency Shower, appropriate First Aid kits,
           neutralizer substances etc.).

           materials  (e.g., acids, and alkalis, organic materials and strong oxidizing

       Standard of utilization and discharging of wastes on location
       Conduct drilling operation according to the design, drilling mud is needed
              before spud.
       Sewage that cannot be recovered shall be put into the pit， do not discharge
       Make full use of waste water. Water in mud pump and water brake must be
                utilized by circulating. Water that is used to wash drill floor, shall be recovered
                 after deposition. The recovery rate of waste water shall reach 85％.

       Disposed  oil  shall be recovered  entirely. Recovery rate  shall be 100％.
